Written for the angular momentum, such as $R\rho v_\phi$, both become conservation laws again, with the geometric factors inside the divergence of the fluxes.

#### 2.8.4 Finite volumes and geometric source terms

The finite volumes update volume averages with fluxes averaged over faces and over the step ([Skinner and Ostriker](#skinner-ostriker-2010) 2010, §7). In cylindrical coordinates the volume element is $R\,dR\,d\phi\,dz$, and with the volumes and areas of 2.8.1 the update reads $\mathbf{Q}^{n+1} = \mathbf{Q}^n - [\Delta t/(R_i\Delta R)](R_{i+1/2}\mathbf{F}_{R,i+1/2} - R_{i-1/2}\mathbf{F}_{R,i-1/2}) - [\Delta t/(R_i\Delta\phi)](\mathbf{F}_{\phi,j+1/2} - \mathbf{F}_{\phi,j-1/2}) - (\Delta t/\Delta z)(\mathbf{F}_{z,k+1/2} - \mathbf{F}_{z,k-1/2}) + \Delta t\,\mathbf{S}$, with the fluxes at $t^{n+1/2}$ and $\mathbf{S}$ the source terms averaged over the cell and the step (eq. 72). Applied to eq. 13 it would update the angular momentum. The radial part of the corresponding update of $\rho v_\phi$ by eq. 14, $-[\Delta t/(R_i^2\Delta R)](R_{i+1/2}^2F_{R,i+1/2} - R_{i-1/2}^2F_{R,i-1/2})$, needs no source term (eq. 73). Away from the origin and for smooth flows it agrees to second order with the update by eq. 11b, which needs the averaged source term $-M_{R\phi}/R = -(\rho v_Rv_\phi - B_RB_\phi)/R$ (eq. 74). The one geometric source term that remains, the average of $M_{\phi\phi}/R$ over the cell and the step (eq. 75), needs estimates centred in time for second order, not averages at $t^n$. Athena advances $\rho$ and $\rho v_\phi$ to the half step by finite volumes and $B_\phi$ by CT. Doing the same for the total pressure would be too costly, so its part comes from the pressures that the Riemann solver returns on the radial faces, averaged over the step, as $\langle P^*/R\rangle \approx (R_{i+1/2}P^*_{i+1/2} + R_{i-1/2}P^*_{i-1/2})/(2R_i^2)$ (eq. 76). Centrifugal balance proved easier to maintain with the analytic acceleration $\mathbf{g} = -\nabla\Phi$ in the momentum equation than with differences of the potential. Gravity then adds $\langle\rho\rangle^{n+1/2}\mathbf{g}(\langle\mathbf{x}\rangle)$, with $\mathbf{g}$ at the volume centroid, which is exact for solid-body rotation at uniform density, where $g \propto R$ (eq. 77). The energy keeps the conservative update by the potential that Athena already had. In the interface states, where gravity enters only the primitive momentum, $\mathbf{g}$ is taken at the area centroids of the faces, whose radii on the faces normal to $\phi$ and $z$ equal that of the volume centroid.

[Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) (2020, §3.2.4) discretise the source terms of eqs. 16 and 17 so that Athena++ conserves angular momentum to machine precision. The source term of eq. 16 is written as $M_{R\phi}/R \approx (R_{i+1/2} - R_{i-1/2})(R_{i+1/2}M_{R\phi,i+1/2} + R_{i-1/2}M_{R\phi,i-1/2})/[(R_{i-1/2} + R_{i+1/2})V_R]$, with $V_R = (R_{i+1/2}^2 - R_{i-1/2}^2)/2$ and the stresses on the radial faces taken from the momentum fluxes of the Riemann solver that update the cell (eq. 18). The discrete equation, flux differences included, is then algebraically identical to the conservative difference of the angular momentum. In spherical-polar coordinates the first source term of eq. 17 takes a similar form, with $V_r = (r_{i+1/2}^3 - r_{i-1/2}^3)/3$. The second becomes $\cot\theta M_{\phi\theta}/r \approx (S_{j+1/2} - S_{j-1/2})(S_{j+1/2}M_{\phi\theta,j+1/2} + S_{j-1/2}M_{\phi\theta,j-1/2})/[r_i(S_{j-1/2} + S_{j+1/2})V_\theta]$, with $S = \sin\theta$, $V_\theta = \cos\theta_{j-1/2} - \cos\theta_{j+1/2}$ and the momentum fluxes on the faces normal to $\theta$ (eq. 19). The source terms of the remaining momentum components take the appropriate volume averages, and general relativity brings coordinate source terms of its own (2.14).

`Coordinates::AddCoordTermsDivergence` adds these terms to the conserved variables at each stage of the integrator, with the weight of the stage and with the primitive variables and fluxes of that stage (`src/task_list/time_integrator.cpp`; 2.6.5). In cylindrical coordinates it adds $\langle 1/R\rangle M_{\phi\phi}$ to the radial momentum, with $\langle 1/R\rangle = \Delta R/V_R = 1/R_i$ and $M_{\phi\phi} = \rho v_\phi^2 + P + (B_R^2 - B_\phi^2 + B_z^2)/2$ from the volume-centred primitive variables and field, where $P = c_s^2\rho$ for an isothermal gas (`src/coordinates/cylindrical.cpp`). It subtracts the term of eq. 18 from the azimuthal momentum. In spherical-polar coordinates the radial momentum gains $\langle 1/r\rangle(M_{\theta\theta} + M_{\phi\phi})$, with $\langle 1/r\rangle = \tfrac{3}{2}(r_{i+1/2}^2 - r_{i-1/2}^2)/(r_{i+1/2}^3 - r_{i-1/2}^3)$ and $M_{\theta\theta} + M_{\phi\phi} = \rho(v_\theta^2 + v_\phi^2) + 2P + B_r^2$ (`src/coordinates/spherical_polar.cpp`). The polar and azimuthal momenta lose radial terms in the form of eq. 18, with the weights $r_{i\pm1/2}^2$. The polar momentum gains $\langle 1/r\rangle\langle\cot\theta\rangle M_{\phi\phi}$, with $\langle\cot\theta\rangle = (\sin\theta_{j+1/2} - \sin\theta_{j-1/2})/V_\theta$, and the azimuthal momentum loses the term of eq. 19, with $\langle 1/r\rangle$ as the factor $1/r_i$, which the polar flux divergence carries as well. Without a polar direction in the mesh, this last term uses the volume-centred stress $M_{\phi\theta} = \rho v_\phi v_\theta - B_\phi B_\theta$ instead. The stresses of viscosity enter these terms as well, unless super time-stepping applies them, in which case `Coordinates::AddCoordTermsDivergence_STS` adds their part (2.11). Athena++ thus evaluates $M_{\phi\phi}$ at each stage from the volume-centred variables, where Athena advances them to the half step and takes the pressure from the fluxes. Gravity is a source term of its own (2.9). **[GK]** With the radial flux difference of `Hydro::AddFluxDivergence`, the term of eq. 18 gives the azimuthal momentum the radial update $-[\Delta t/(R_iV_R)](R_{i+1/2}^2F_{i+1/2} - R_{i-1/2}^2F_{i-1/2})$, which is eq. 73 of Athena, as $V_R = R_i\Delta R$.

#### 2.8.5 Constrained transport

CT carries over to cylindrical coordinates in its integral form, as the conservation of magnetic flux through the faces of a cell, which preserves the divergence constraint by construction ([Skinner and Ostriker](#skinner-ostriker-2010) 2010, §8). With $\partial_t\mathbf{B} + \nabla\times\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}} = 0$ and $\boldsymbol{\mathcal{E}} = -\mathbf{v}\times\mathbf{B}$ (eq. 78), the theorem of Stokes on each face updates the area-averaged normal component of the field by the electric fields averaged along the edges and over the step (eqs. 79 to 81). On a radial face, $B_R$ changes by $-\Delta t/(R_{i\pm1/2}\Delta\phi)$ times the difference of $\mathcal{E}_z$ between the edges at $\phi_{j+1/2}$ and $\phi_{j-1/2}$, plus $\Delta t/\Delta z$ times that of $\mathcal{E}_\phi$ between the edges at $z_{k+1/2}$ and $z_{k-1/2}$. On an azimuthal face, $B_\phi$ changes by $\Delta t/\Delta R$ times the difference of $\mathcal{E}_z$ between $R_{i+1/2}$ and $R_{i-1/2}$, minus $\Delta t/\Delta z$ times that of $\mathcal{E}_R$ between $z_{k+1/2}$ and $z_{k-1/2}$. On a face normal to $z$, $B_z$ changes by $-\Delta t/(R_i\Delta R)$ times the difference of $R\mathcal{E}_\phi$ between $R_{i+1/2}$ and $R_{i-1/2}$, plus $\Delta t/(R_i\Delta\phi)$ times that of $\mathcal{E}_R$ between $\phi_{j+1/2}$ and $\phi_{j-1/2}$. The face averages are the primary representation of the field. The cell-centred field averages them, with the weights $R_{i\pm1/2}/(2R_i)$ for $B_R$, the average of second order in cylindrical coordinates, and with plain averages for $B_\phi$ and $B_z$ (eqs. 82a to 82c). Through consistency relations, these averages tie the Godunov fluxes of the cell-centred field to the corner electric fields, as in [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005). **[GK]** Eq. 79c as printed lacks the radii in the difference of $\mathcal{E}_\phi$, which it gives as $\Delta t(\mathcal{E}_{\phi,i+1/2} - \mathcal{E}_{\phi,i-1/2})/(R_i\Delta R)$. The theorem of Stokes needs the lengths $R_{i\pm1/2}\Delta\phi$ of the azimuthal edges, which eq. 79a uses for the radial faces, and without them the divergence of the field would not be preserved.

The main change to the CTU+CT algorithm of [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2005) (2005) concerns the corner value of $\mathcal{E}_z$, which combines gradients along $\phi$ at different radii (§8.2). For $\mathcal{E}_R$ and $\mathcal{E}_\phi$ the gradients combined share their curvature, which cancels. As in 2.3.5, $\mathcal{E}_z$ is integrated to the corner from the four neighbouring face centres and averaged. At $(i-1/2, j-1/2)$ the average of the four face values is corrected by $R_{i-1/2}\Delta\phi/8$ times the difference of the azimuthal gradients below and above the corner, and by $\Delta R/8$ times that of the radial gradients on either side (eqs. 83, 84). Each gradient is taken from the upwind side, which the direction of the mass flux through the face centre decides, as for $\mathcal{E}^c_z$ (eq. 85). The azimuthal gradients are the differences between the face and centre values of $\mathcal{E}_z$ divided by $R_i\Delta\phi/2$ in cell $i$ and by $R_{i-1}\Delta\phi/2$ in cell $i-1$ (eqs. 86a, 86b), so that $\Delta\phi$ cancels but the ratios of the radii remain and must enter the algorithm. The scheme does not depend on the Riemann solver.

Athena++ applies CT with the areas of the faces and the lengths of the edges of 2.8.1, as `Field::CT` does in any coordinates (2.3.4). It carries the electric fields to the corners without geometric factors, with the same differences between face and centre values in every coordinate system, rather than with the ratios of radii of eqs. 86a and 86b (`src/field/calculate_corner_e.cpp`, `Field::ComputeCornerE`; 2.3.5). It also interpolates the cell-centred field linearly to the volume centre (2.3.4), where Athena weights $B_R$ by the radii of the faces.

#### 2.8.6 The integrator of Athena in cylindrical coordinates

[Skinner and Ostriker](#skinner-ostriker-2010) (2010, §3) recall the steps of Athena, from the interface states through fluxes of first order, CT of the states, transverse corrections and fluxes of second order to the updates of the field by CT and of the other variables by finite volumes. The Riemann solvers need no change to their wave speeds, eigenfunctions or fluxes, and the tests use HLLE, HLLC, HLLD and the solver of Roe (2.5). In cylindrical coordinates the six-solve CTU+CT of [Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) (2008) takes thirteen steps (§9). First, the source terms of first order are computed at $t^n$ in conservative and primitive form: the geometric terms (eq. 75, and eq. 35 for the primitive variables; 2.8.3), gravity by eq. 77, and the source terms of MHD that follow from the divergence constraint (2.6.4). Reconstruction with characteristic evolution gives the interface states, and the primitive source terms of each direction are added to them, and the Riemann solver gives the fluxes of first order. The corner electric fields follow from these fluxes and from the cell-centred field at $t^n$, $\mathcal{E}_z$ by eqs. 84 and 85, and CT advances the face fields by half a step. The gradients of the transverse fluxes and the source terms of the transverse directions then correct the interface states. The density and momentum are advanced by half a step with the first-order fluxes and source terms, the half-step face fields are averaged to the cell centres by eq. 82, and the two give the cell-centred electric field at the half step. The corrected states yield the fluxes of second order and, with that electric field, the corner fields of second order. For the source terms at the half step, the density and azimuthal momentum are advanced once more with the new fluxes, and the total pressure follows from eq. 76. Together with $B_\phi$ at the half step they give eq. 75. Gravity in the momentum uses the density and $\mathbf{g}$, and gravity in the energy the mass fluxes on the faces. The full step then updates mass, momentum and energy by finite volumes and the face fields by CT (eq. 79), the cell-centred field follows from eq. 82, and the next step obeys the Courant condition with the width $R_i\Delta\phi$ along $\phi$, as the Riemann solvers return linear speeds. Hydrodynamics needs neither the source terms of MHD nor the magnetic parts of the geometric terms nor electric fields. Compared with the Cartesian code, the interface states change substantially along $R$, slightly along $\phi$ and not at all along $z$. The finite volumes gain their scale factors and geometric source terms, the source terms differ between the primitive and the conservative form, and the total pressure enters differently at $t^n$ and at the half step. The corner fields and the update by CT take the scale factors of 2.8.5.

## 3. Software architecture

Nine subjects group the software architecture. `plan.md` lists them, with the steps that write them.

[Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) (2020, §1) explain three decisions that shape the framework of Athena++. First, the mesh is abstract and independent of the physics. The discretisations of the variables, such as volume averages at cell centres, area averages on faces and point values at cell centres or vertices, are built and stored on it, and AMR, boundary conditions and parallelisation by decomposing the domain are implemented for these variables without reference to any physics. New coordinates and new physics therefore work with AMR in any geometry at once, and mesh and physics can be developed apart, as in a version of the framework on Kokkos ([Edwards, Trott and Sunderland](#edwards-et-al-2014) 2014) for heterogeneous machines with GPUs. Second, the mesh is refined by blocks ([Stout et al.](#stout-et-al-1997) 1997) rather than by overlapping patches after [Berger and Oliger](#berger-oliger-1984) (1984), as in Athena, PLUTO and Enzo. With patches, a refined region is covered by several levels, on which quantities computed from the conserved variables, the temperature for instance, may differ, and source terms that depend on them, such as cooling or reaction networks, then drive the levels apart. The overlap between the domains of the processes of the Message Passing Interface (MPI) on different levels also grows complex. In Athena++ each point of the domain belongs to exactly one level, and the levels communicate only through their boundaries, which simplifies the code and improves its performance and scaling. FLASH ([Fryxell et al.](#fryxell-et-al-2000) 2000) also refines by blocks, with the library PARAMESH ([MacNeice et al.](#macneice-et-al-2000) 2000), but Athena++ uses a framework of its own, to support variables on faces, as its MHD requires, to depend on fewer libraries and to gain performance at the cost of generality. Third, the steps of an algorithm, including the sends and receives of MPI, are not executed in a fixed order but assembled into lists of tasks, each of which runs once the tasks it depends on are complete. Computation can then overlap communication, which improves scaling on very many processors, and a calculation chooses its physics by the composition of its task lists. Separate regions can even run separate lists, for example a particle-in-cell code for the collisionless corona above an accretion disc in MHD ([Miller and Stone](#miller-stone-2000) 2000). Libraries such as Legion and Charm++ offer such dynamic execution among much else, but Athena++ implements only the scheduling it needs, in a few thousand lines of its own.

### 3.2 Arrays, the mesh and its blocks

All data of the grid lie in arrays of the template class `AthenaArray`, of one to six dimensions (`src/athena_arrays.hpp`, `AthenaArray`). The data occupy one contiguous block of memory, and the operator of function call addresses them with the last index fastest, so that `A(n,k,j,i)` is the element `i + N1*(j + N2*(k + N3*n))` of an array of size $N_4 \times N_3 \times N_2 \times N_1$. Loops with `i` innermost thus run through memory in order. An array records whether it is empty, allocated, or a shallow slice of another array. Allocated memory is set to zero. Copying an array copies its data, assigning one requires the target to be allocated with the same size already, and moving one hands over its data. Arrays may be allocated after their construction (`NewAthenaArray`) and released (`DeleteAthenaArray`). `SwapAthenaArray` exchanges the data of two arrays of equal size without copying them, and `InitWithShallowSlice` and `ShallowSlice3DToPencil` make views into part of another array, such as a range of its variables or one line of cells along $x_1$, again without copying. The type `Real` of the data is `double` by default and `float` when the code is configured for single precision, and `SIMD_WIDTH` sets the width of the vector lanes from the instruction set that the compiler targets (`src/athena.hpp`). The same header declares the structures for the size of a region and the logical location of a block in the tree of refinement, the containers `FaceField` and `EdgeField` for fields on faces and edges, the enumerations of indices and directions, and the types of the functions that users may enrol (3.6).
